ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e3575	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Members of the Federal Government! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The Federal Government has given an opinion on the draft harmonisation of pensions in Austria. I believe that today is a convenient time to discuss some of these key points, because, of course, there is still time for the parliamentary negotiation of the Pensi-Unsreform to discuss and make some corrections that we feel are urgently necessary. No one in Austria doubts the need for pension reforms. This is, I believe, a good basis for discussing also the question of the future of the pension system. I believe that there is also a huge need for reliability, fairness and justice in pensions in Austria, because it is known that pensions are not only financed by contributions, but also there is a state grant to pensions. If you take this need for fairness, justice and reliability seriously and try to discuss and light up the pension reform against this background, you have to say something about reliability on the one hand. Mr Bundeskanzler, you said before the 2002 elections that there was no further need for change in pensions, because the 2000 century reform was entirely sufficient. – For a very long time, this century reform has not taken place, considering that there has been a massive pension reduction reform last year and that further cuts should be made in this year under the title of pension harmonisation. We must therefore not be surprised if many, especially young people, have already lost faith in the pension system in our country. I think this is a very serious matter, because the pension system lives on the fact that the people who are at work today are paying their contributions in the legitimate hope of getting a fair pension sometime. Mr Bundeskanzler, what you have presented does not correspond to this expectation in many areas, because many parts of this pension reform are neither fair nor fair! There is therefore an enormous need for change. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] The real negative surprise is that last year, when there were massive pension cuts, you said that there will now be a pension harmonisation – which means a harmonisation of pension systems – but you did not say that this pension harmonisation leads to further reductions, especially among workers and employees. But what is available today is another reduction in pensions due to this harmonisation of pensions! I will address some of these examples in a moment. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Unfortunately, the main sufferers of this pension harmonisation will be women, women due to their different employment biographies, because they will suffer from this system of pension harmonisation quite massive pension reductions. [[Abg. May. Molterer: Incorrect!]] [[Abg. May. Molterer: That's just wrong!]] Nor will the billing of child replacement periods, Mr. Klubobmann Molterer, be enough to bet these massive pension cuts. If the 15 best years are used for the calculation of pensions, the question of the billing of child rearing periods plays a much lower role than when the income of employment of the entire life is counted. [[Abg. Scheibner: Why do women have such low pensions?]] [[Abg. May. Molterer: SPÖ-Pensionsmodel!]] Mr Klubobmann Molterer, you will not be able to conceal the fact that the main sufferers of this pension harmonisation will unfortunately be the women in Austria. And that's extremely unfair! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] [[Abg. Steibl: That's not true!]] Two examples of this. First: There are 400 000 people in Austria who do part-time work. Most of these parttime workers are women. This will lead to massive collapses at the pension level in life-long calculation. Secondly, The difficult work regulations you propose are not very precise in that they do not de facto exclude women. Do you really think there are no women in Austria who work hard? - I can't imagine that. There must also be the possibility for women to have a heavy-worker scheme. This is an absolute command of fairness! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] The second group of the main losers will be all those people who are seasonal workers – be it in tourism, whether it be in the construction industry, where all the losses that arise due to the recurring times of unemployment will in the future be fully reduced to the pension level. If these seasonal workers are women, this means that women who do seasonal work will be double affected by this pension reform. [[Abg. Dr. Partik-Pablé: Why do you see everything so negative? The great regret!]] Federal Chancellor! It is therefore true that this harmonisation of pensions will double the loss of pensions in 2003 and, unfortunately, according to the calculation of the Chamber of Labour, it is expected that the loss of pensions will sometimes amount to 20 percent and more. This is neither fair nor fair, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. [[Conjugation with the SPÖ and the Abg. Oils.]] [[Abg. May. Molterer: This is not true!]] A harmonised pension system that creates more justice would have at least once equal contributions and equal benefits. But this principle is also not fulfilled by this proposal for the harmonisation of pensions. There are still different levels of contribution and different benefits. [[Abg. May. Molterer: Proposal from the social partners!]] And these are not justified in many areas. Mr Molterer, tell me the following: Why, for example, do officials have to pay higher contributions to a nurse, a gendarm, a penitentiary, even though he or she will receive a pension lower than the maximum pension of the ASVG? Why do they also have to pay a pension contribution when they are in retirement? – There is no reasonable reason for this. The only reasonable thing would be the same contributions that lead to the same benefits! This is the right to a harmonised pension system. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! One has the impression that, in all cases, the difficulties in developing a harmonised pension system – and nobody argues that this is one of the most difficult and complex materials – have been forgotten on individual groups in our society. [[Abg. Dr. Partik-Pablé: I can't hear that anymore!]] Imagine what it means when it comes to pensions of 700 € to 20 percent reductions! Imagine that after long working life people will not have the opportunity to use their heavy work regulations! Imagine what this means especially for women who already have very low pensions today! These reductions will put them all in the compensation allowance. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! If you say that you can adapt to the new pension system and try to compensate for losses through private provision, I tell you: People today 48 or 49 years old and entering the new system will no longer have much time to build up an additional private provision, which will then also lead to a reasonable pension. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! There are quite reasonable traditions. In 1997, when there was a reform of civil servants' pensions, the social situation was taken into account in the case of losses. So there has been a staggering, it has been taken into account the incomes of officials. I therefore ask you: Why should what was good and right for officials in 1997 not also be valid for workers and employees, so as to prevent more and more people from being pushed into poverty in old age? Ladies and gentlemen of the government groups! Take the time to repair before we in Parliament deal with this problem in depth! It would make sense to create fairness, justice and reliability.

Walter Hallstein, CDU, President of the EEC Commission 1963, then spoke quite clearly and ambiguously for Turkey as a full member “one day”. By now 40 years have passed. If today the question arises: Should Turkey/can be included as a member?, one should of course say: No, no, no, Turkey has not fulfilled the usual conditions, and the Union is currently having other concerns: enlargement from 15 to 25, Bulgaria, Romania, Croatia, the rest of the Balkan countries; the next budget period, which will start in 2007 and end in 2012. – All this would make it impossible to cross this bridge today. [[President Mag. Prammer shall take over the chairmanship.]] But we do not have to exceed this bridge today. Today we are dealing with a completely different issue, namely whether the Council will decide in December to open negotiations with Turkey – negotiations, which, as everyone talks to, will certainly take a decade, and I think this is realistic, even in view of the EU budget period we are talking about here. And the Council has already decided in a ambiguous way. 1999 – a SPÖ politician was German Chancellor – the Council in Helsinki said it literally: ‘Turkish is a candidate country to become a member of the Union on the basis of the same criteria applicable to the other candidate countries.’ This is the Council's official position! – There is a little at stake here, namely the entire credibility of the Union of candidate countries. Now I know, there is Pro and Kontra on this question. It is not so easy then to give an answer under the line to draw a balance. However, the Council has already de facto drawn this balance with its opinions in December 1999 and two years ago in Copenhagen. So I would warn very much that I would easily take different positions from those of the time. And above all, I attach great importance to Turkey being treated the same as other former candidate countries. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] It cannot be said that, at the time of negotiations, the countries concerned would have met all the criteria – no, otherwise, it would no longer have needed negotiations. [[President Mag. Prammer gives the bell sign.]] Austria has failed to meet these criteria, Ireland certainly not, Poland not. But over time these countries have fulfilled them! So if someone were to teach a little further education... – no, I wouldn't say so. – The counter-arguments stand today and have also been in the room in the last few weeks; they have been expressed by colleagues Cap and others as well. As far as the pro-arguments are concerned, I would just like to say one sentence, Madam President: Albert Rohan, ex-Secretary-General of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, has written a report on Turkey as a candidate. [[The speaker keeps a book entitled “Turkish Turkey in Europe” up to date.]] This is worth reading, essentially contains the pro arguments. “The Economist” is also clear in its statement: [[Abg. Dr. Cap: Who is funding this?]] ‘Why Europe must say yes to Turkey’. [[Abg. Dr. Cap: These are the British who finance this!]] You don't have to join this, but you have to study and take these arguments seriously. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e8438	Mr President. We are dealing with the collective report of the Committee on Petitions and Citizens' Initiatives. It has already been pointed out. The Mariazellerbahn leads from the capital of St. Pölten through a beautiful area, through the Pielachtal, then further to the Alps to Mariazell, and it is of course an important concern that this railway operation continues to be maintained. On the one hand, it is an important link for the commuters and students. On the other hand, it has been particularly evident in recent years that it is becoming more and more an attractive railway in the area of tourism. That's what the numbers show. In recent years, the frequency has increased enormously in the tourist sector. 17 municipalities and the population of the Pielachal, but also of the Mariazellerland, have made a very clear commitment to this traditional railway link in recent years. As to the accusation made by Mr Heinzl, I must point out that the petition states that neither the federal government nor the state of Lower Austria directly invested in the infrastructure, which has been the Minister of Transport since the Minister of Transport, was in the mid-1990s. That's just not right! In the area of infrastructure, measures were very likely to have been taken. Now I would like to point out in particular that, as part of this petition, opinions have been received from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, but also from the State of Lower Austria. In fact, the timetable is clearly defined how the Mariazellerbahn will continue in the future. First of all, the important finding is that the Mariazellerbahn – and this is also seen on the part of the country of Lower Austria – is an important backbone of the regions of Pielachtal and Mariazellerland for transport and economic policy. That's a very clear confession. The Land of Lower Austria has also recognized this and has pointed out it several times to those responsible for the federal government, but also to the ÖBB. The country has repeatedly requested the necessary measures to ensure a permanent scheduled regular operation and has also offered to find common solutions. I think that an important, therefore essential, step was taken in December 2003, i.e. in the previous year, when an infrastructure agreement between the Federal Government and the State of Lower Austria was concluded, in which the Federal Government has now given an inventory guarantee for the next five years. This Treaty also agreed that a working group should be set up. This working group will examine the conditions and discuss the possibility of retracement on the Mariazellerbahn, which is undoubtedly necessary for the preservation. Today, by chance, a discussion in the district of Hofstetten is in the Pielachtal, in which this working group informs the population, thus giving it an interim report. We all assume that the Mariazellerbahn is long-term secured. If a positive opinion of the working group is ultimately made when the work is completed, it is already agreed in this Treaty that the Costs for the necessary infrastructure measures in the course of further expansion 80 percent of the federal government and 20 percent of the state of Lower Austria takes over. Dear ladies and gentlemen! Finally, the condition is that the operation is maintained by an operator. We know that this is not possible on the part of the federal government and would be classified as distorting competition. I therefore think that decisions will be taken within the next few years, i.e. within the framework of this five-year quota, as long as the stock guarantee is in place. We all expect it, and all the responsibilities are convinced that the Mariazellerbahn continues to drive and is securely secure.

I would like to address two current aspects here in order to show you that many of these dangers remain completely in the dark. The first is available on the Internet at http://science.orf.at. Why has it not been approved? – Because French scientists have found that, after feeding tests in rats, changes in the kidneys that lymph systems et cetera may occur. Clearly, it is a huge danger potential that needs to be seen, namely that genetically modified feed can cause changes in animals. There are now 17 varieties in the EU catalogue. This means in principle that they could be grown throughout the European Union. First of all, there is consensus, but it must also be legalised: ensuring non-genotechnic agriculture through the creation of non-genotechnic regions and the legal framework for them, so that countries are given the opportunity to implement such initiatives on the basis of a single federal directive. The second, of course, concerns liability in accordance with the 'polluter pays' principle. – Minister of State! In this respect, the current legislative novel is not clear enough. There is criticism of this because it is very complicated rules. I urge you to use the uniform regime for the Alpine countries, which is the German liability model, which is negotiated in intensive discussions with your German colleague Jürgen Trittin in Potsdam. That would be very important. Absolute transparency in the case of releases, corresponding location registers with ground-specific information must be enshrined in the law so that all relevant measures of public interest can be taken in advance. Above all, it must be an explicit ban on genetically modified organisms in ecologically sensitive areas – in Natura 2000 areas, but also, of course, in regions established in Austria as bioregions. There is already a lot of such regions. Finally, we also consider a ban on experimental releases necessary. What we're supposed to do is risk research, but not to take a risk in the sense of release. That wouldn't be useful! Mr. Federal Minister, what are you doing? – From my point of view, too little, lip recognition and statements, which I welcome, such as the Charter of Genetic Freedom, which you initiated here in summer, but in view of which I wonder where your concrete legislative measures remain? Where do your proposals remain within the framework of the genetic engineering law novels? It's missing at all corners and ends! They have moved back to the position that the concrete co-existence measures are intended to regulate countries at national level. But that is absolutely too little! If Landesrat Stockinger from Upper Austria, together with his Tuscan colleague, tries to raise an alliance of non-genotechnic regions in Europe, and says that there must be a right of self-determination of the regions – as he has, for example, represented at the Welser Messe, which you have opened – I expect you to strengthen these initiatives at the national level at the federal level. That is absolutely necessary, Minister of State! In conclusion: We need the protection of non-genotechnic seed production in Austria in order to be able to continue successfully these initiatives towards non-genotechnic regions. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e14601	Madam President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! Back to the petitions; I am dealing with the mobile petition today. The topic of electromagnetic radiation has been working on us, the ÖVP, since there have been transmission masts. Just ten years ago, the first GSM masts were built in Vorarlberg. Today, with nearly seven million mobile customers in Austria, our commitment to ensuring safety in the population is as high as at the time. The problem is that this issue is being used more and more to promote populism, more than it may have been before. I would therefore like to make a few points before the debate is over. Mobile operators in Austria comply with the values of the International Commission for Non-ionizing Radiation Protection, which are also recommended by the World Health Organisation. The internationally recognised limit values of the World Health Organisation and the EU are the electromagnetic power density of 10 watts per square metre for UMTS, 9 watts per square metre for GSM 1 800 and 4.5 watts for GSM 900 megahertz. These limit values are absolutely binding and are also subject to strict verification and verification by the Telecommunications Authority in the event of suspicion. The international limit values shall be reviewed on a regular basis. A study presented by the WHO in Turkey in June 2004 confirmed that the limit values were set correctly. A British test report is also concluded. it has not been possible to fear a health hazard from mobile phones or mobile phones. A survey of mobile Immissions in the Styria was also carried out, after all, for over three years. The highest measured value in the 900 megahertz frequency band was only 0.0125 watts per square metre of power flow. This is saying and writing 3 600 times lower than the international limit value! We are thus far below the limit in Austria, which would also include a 50-fold security. The demand for an Immission Scatter for the population is already a reality. In addition, in Austria, there is a contact point for communication with avians, the Forum Mobilkommunikation commissions ongoing studies on electromagnetic radiation. The companies themselves also take great care to give the population a speech and an answer. I really do not understand how to speak of a lack of information. We have sufficient legal provisions to protect against electromagnetic danger. The Telecommunications Act, which we jointly adopted last summer, regulates the establishment and operation of the transmission facilities, taking into account the protection of the human being, and also the topic of mast-sharing is included here. Pushing the topic artificially now is pure SPÖ populism, leading to an uncertainty of the population and to economic damage to businesses. I therefore propose that the facts be examined first. Before “Round Tables” unnecessarily develop new laws that have long existed, we will continue to drive the way of innovation. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e16376	Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The marriage has largely lost its monopoly and is only one of many forms of life. Especially the nonmarital community has since become a mass phenomenon. As Professor Prisching also pointed out, the reasons for this are varied: Many also see the longing for family silenced in a community of life, and many simply shy away from marriage because they see marriage as that – and in this point I should correspond to one of your studies, Mrs. State Secretary – which she is despite all the means of resolution, namely, a covenant for life, and do not trust to give a lifelong guarantee. Whatever the reasons are: The fact is that the non-marital community has become the second most common form of life. However, legislation ignores this fact and does not provide sufficient framework for this form of partnership. Compatriots, even though they have been living together for decades, are legally regarded as strangers. Only in individual peripheral areas are they recognised, but often only to the detriment, such as the emergency aid in which the partner income is used for calculation, although there is no maintenance claim. While life mates live like spouses, have a common household, work together, sometimes raise children together, take care of each other in the event of illness, and provide assistance for each other. However, when it comes to separation, there are serious problems as regards the sharing of common assets and liabilities. The courts have developed legal structures, but these are only economic and non-family-law. In practice, this means that family work is only insufficiently taken into account. This, of course, falls on the head of those who have invested a lot in the community, whether by family work or by contesting household costs, and here in most cases the women have the bamboo. There is no legal maintenance claim, even if the partner gives up the profession in agreement with the other, in order to devote himself to child rearing. I think that this problem is likely to worsen in the future, because child money and child protection fall apart and mothers are sleeping directly into the unemployed from child money, but do not get into emergency aid, because, as I said, a fictitious partner income is charged. If a companion dies, the state's right to return comes before the other gets something. The list of discrimination can be continued for a long time, but it can be seen that there is a massive need for regulation in any form whatsoever. Some might be linked to a form of partnership registration, some to the mere fact of the community. Especially acute is the need for action in homosexual communities, which do not have the alternative of marriage. I hope that the excuse from your series in this direction was not only a cheap approach to certain target groups, but that you are really interested in modern family law.

What's this about? – It is about – we have already discussed this today – that sign language is still not recognized as a language, although everyone has to be clear that sign language is a language. Languages are recognized in Austria, only the sign language is not. We were first promised that the sign language as part of the law on equal treatment for persons with disabilities should be established as a right. But now this point has been deleted from the Disability Equality Act – and we have been promised that the anchoring of the sign language will be recorded as a language in the Federal Constitutional Act, and that is also my request. Ladies and gentlemen! I may repeat now, but that's important so you can finally understand what it's all about. It is about the fact that deaf people must not be forced to learn the voice, just so that we can hearers understand it. Sign language must be recognized as language! It is our duty as a hearer to acquire the sign language so that we can communicate with the deaf as well. It is not the duty of the deaf to learn the voice, for that is another language, a foreign language. Your language is the sign language! There is also the desire of many to have implants inserted into the head, so that you may hear a little bit. Ladies and gentlemen, it is everyone's own thing and every woman's own whether she can and wants to do this operation – regardless of the fact that it is far from certain that this will actually do something. This simply does not work – and it also suffers from the quality of the class! The children are thus forced into a worse education; their vocabulary is also accordingly restricted, because the sign language consists of a completely different vocabulary than the language spoken. You finally have to hear this, ladies and gentlemen. Allow sign language in Austria to be recognized as a language – and not to be treated as a side-by-side by saying: It is not so many who speak this language! – It is not about how many of them are, but about the right to their own language! A few years ago, a global congress of deaf people was held in Hofburg. There was also a large exhibition of pictures. I went there, saw a picture and wanted to know who it is and what it costs. I first understood what it means to be deaf. I was the only hearer there, and I couldn't ask anyone because I didn't control the sign language. I was there only two hours, but you can't imagine what this was for me: to be somewhere, to want to ask or to have something and not to ask, because you don't understand the language you speak yourself. For me, this situation was limited to two hours, but deaf people are in this situation for a lifetime, which I have already felt as a stress situation for two hours. Let us stop creating disabilities by forbidding languages, but let us remove disability by allowing sign language as a language, because this is a progress for deaf people, and this must finally happen in Austria too! - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e17912	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! In Austria, about 10,000 people live with innate or acquired deafness, and many of them cannot understand spoken words and do not understand in the spoken language. Mrs Haidlmayr has already said: These people have major problems in dealing with their daily lives, which can very often also be attributed to these lack of communication possibilities. For example, children who have been born deafly or who have been deafened before the acquisition of language are at risk because of the lack of hearing impressions in their voice development and thus in their social and cognitive development. To outline this whole problem shortly, I would like to quote Helen Keller, who once said: “Blindness separates from things and deafness from men.” I believe that the quote of this woman, who has been deafened and blinded in the early childhood and who has nevertheless completed a university degree, is, in my opinion, exactly the core of the problem. It is very easy to overcome this separation, to seek ways out of this isolation or not to create isolation at all. There has been much discussion and discussion about how to prevent the risks of development caused by deafness. This morning, colleague Heinisch-Hosek has already addressed as early as possible the provision of equipment as possible and intensive early logos in order to allow for a semi-normal language development. - That's a way. Another way is to embed deaf children into a sign language speaking environment. Thanks to the excellent work of Austrian linguists at the University of Graz, the sign language has become instructive and learningable, and this has also provided the basis for a comprehensive use of the sign language. Our task – that was also said by my previous speaker – is now to anchor the right to use the sign language. For this reason, we have also tabled and believe in a resolution to provide the basis for necessary reforms in the field of early promotion, education and training and in many other areas of life. These reforms – and I trust to say so today – will bring forth many discussions. Without presuming this, however, I would like to warn of some misunderstandings. An error that is widespread is the one that is meant to exclude sign language promotion and vocal support. This view is wrong and outdated. As we have noted in our survey, neuropsychological and linguistic studies confirm that both systems support each other. Speaking and movement belong together, and for the benefit of the child, this circumstance can be used perfectly. That is to say, the principle of one-sided support is wrong, but the principle of bilinguality applies. In this way, almost every deaf child can acquire a little vocal competence, and this is always necessary and important for social integration. Experience shows that sign language is primarily used and maintained in the community of the deaf, that by interpreters God is offered to support, but otherwise always limited to the closest environment of the persons concerned. That, I fear, will remain the same, and that is why I would like to pay tribute to the principle of Bilinguality. I would also like to thank you very much for translating this contribution into the sign language and hope that we will soon be able to introduce the corresponding reforms.

In the Federal Republic of Germany, 50 percent of those in detention are released from prison and 92 percent of those in detention are released from prison in Switzerland. These two comparisons are therefore of relevance, because the cultural context in these two countries with Austria is quite similar to the legal system, the enforcement, the rate of crime and the development of crime and, if you wish, the cultural context. I would also say that Austria would certainly be no less secure or an uncertain country than Switzerland or Germany. In many areas Austria is a much safer country than these two countries. There are also lower values for crime rates in Austria than in Switzerland. Nevertheless, the number of conditional redundancies is 20 per cent, 50 per cent in Germany and 92 per cent in Switzerland, and - I repeat it - 92 per cent. As a rule, the most important argument is why there is no conditional redundancie that led the General Prevention to the meeting. This justification is repeatedly stressed: Early dismissal is not possible because of the general prevention. To keep other people from criminal acts – others! – those who sit must sit until the last day. This kind of deterrence does not work in the long run, but at most it works in the probability of being caught. That is why the whole argument about general prevention is simple and simply wrong. We therefore propose four essential points in the reform of conditional redundancies in our own initiative, which is here today and which the Committee on Justice can then deal with after it has been allocated. First point: Consider whether a penalty must continue to be executed in order to prevent others – not the one sitting – from criminal acts – that is the general prevention that has already been mentioned, as in Switzerland and in Germany in the matter of conditional dismissal, should no longer play a role. – This is the first point, which is a very essential one, because that is the main argument for preventing conditional redundancies in Austria. Second point: After two-thirds of the penalty has been fined, a condition must be released. There should be exceptions only in cases where there is an increased risk of relapse and in which the increased risk of relapse threatens to lead to serious acts of violence or public offences. – It is very important to me to stress this in order not to be drawn to the blueness, where it may then mean that every serious criminal we leave out earlier. Third point in the proposed measures: No longer the execution courts should decide on conditional redundancies, namely the courts that also judge, but criminal commissions, i.e. those who have worked and had to do with the prisoner, such as prosecutors, execution staff, social workers in appeal aid. Fourth and also essential point: Even in those risk groups in which the conditional dismissal of a particular consideration is required, there should be a conditional dismissal after five sixths of the penalty, because only then can probation aid be ordered. This is my last information to you, ladies and gentlemen, because it hardly knows anyone, as negligently, I would almost say that the legislator is dealing with criminal offences. [[Abg. This is true even of the club people who are not all-knowing, for otherwise they would sometimes talk wrong if they knew more. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] [[Abg. If someone is locked up in Austria – I say it completely banal – and he sits until the last day – you have heard it, 80 percent of those who sit, do it, then he will be released, and then everything is over. Then there is no parole aid and no follow-up, except that one wants voluntary assistance, but for that the funds are also reduced. ParlaMint-AT_2004-09-22-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00076_d7e18447	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Ladies and gentlemen! We heard: At the request of the Greens, the resulting redundancies should now be extended. This is justified, among other things, by the dramatic increase in the number of prisoners in Austria. But it's not that easy to say: If we have too many prisoners, let's let them go again, let's release them! Our foreign exchange is: There may be a conditional dismissal only if assistance is guaranteed by the probation aid. We must not forget that 30 percent of the foreign prisoners come from abroad, and it is difficult for these persons to implement the parole aid, because the main task of this form of enforcement is to provide psychosocial assistance to those in the labour market. The perpetrators are to be prepared for their release in order to be better able to exist in the community in which society exists. Ladies and gentlemen! Austria has really created a flexible system. It offers the possibility of returning to those who have family, residence and employment. This is not always the case in the case of foreigners, and that is exactly where the problem lies. We are also in favour of a targeted conditional dismissal, with the provision of assistance by means of probation aid. In the individual case, however, it is necessary to consider whether a conditional dismissal is at all reasonable. In principle, it is welcome that prisoners will be released early, and it is better to support them in integration than to keep them in prison until the last day and not to know how they then fit into society. Unfortunately, the crimes of both organised gang deputies and foreign offenders are increasing extremely. These perpetrators, however, are excluded from the possibility of a parole aid, as they often fail to meet the criteria mentioned above. Thus, the implementation of parole aid remains pure theory among these persons. It is therefore important to ensure that conditional redundancies are made only by means of probation aid. The difficulties with the foreign perpetrators must be resolved in criminal proceedings, and the resulting redundancies must be examined in individual cases.
